It adds no different judgment or independent controlling rule.

Justice Thomas dissents, joined by Justice Scalia. They read §401(d) within subsection (a)'s certification of discharges: applicant identifies the obligated person, and the listed federal requirements constrain the broader State-law phrase. Residual flow addresses diversion rather than an established violation caused by a triggering discharge. The Government's alternative needs a demonstrated connection, which the mere existence of construction and tailrace discharges does not supply. They also reject reliance on deference without a statutory ambiguity and an agency interpretation resolving the precise issue; §121.2(a)(4)'s discharge language is substantial contrary regulatory text, while subsection (a)(3)'s activity assurance does not decide the entire conditioning question. Independently, they would implement designated uses through their corresponding protective criteria rather than enforce a use designation as a freestanding condition. The absence of an alleged criteria violation matters on that path, though they do not claim all criteria must be numerical or abolish valid existing-use protection. The mandatory effect of certification terms on a federal license strengthens their narrower reading of Congress's allocation of licensing authority. They find no actual issued FERC condition in conflict, and would reverse this condition's federal authorization and remand without issuing a license, ordering construction or invalidating all separate state water powers.

### 1994-06-06 — [Nichols v. United States; No.92-8556; 511 U.S.738.](../records/Nichols_v_United_States_merits_1994-06-06.md#public-projection)

O'Connor announces the judgment and writes a plurality opinion joined by Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas. They would permit a Scott-valid uncounseled misdemeanor conviction to enhance punishment for a later counseled offense generally, because the new sentence punishes the new offense and does not retrospectively change the earlier fine. Traditional sentencing consideration of prior conduct supports that distinction. Williams v. New York supplies the breadth of lawful sentencing information; McMillan addresses its own sentencing-fact procedure; neither validates inaccurate information or a conviction unconstitutional at its inception. Burgett and Tucker retain their protections against unconstitutional predicates. The four would expressly overrule Baldasar insofar as it restricts this use, based on its fractured and conflicting relation to Scott, competing accounts of its reach and the new-offense principle. Confusion alone is not a general license to discard a divided decision, and lack of reliance on a fixed future sentence does not alone resolve stare decisis. They separately reject a general due-process duty to warn at the misdemeanor plea about possible enhancement for a future offense. That position does not resolve actual counsel waiver, indigency or warnings otherwise required for the plea and present punishment. The broad use, overruling and general-warning propositions each have four votes.

Souter concurs in the judgment. The automatic-enhancement question remains substantial, but the Guidelines offer a materially different setting. The conviction is presumptive sentencing information that the defendant may meaningfully challenge for reliability and relevance, with significant overrepresentation addressed through the lawful §4A1.3 departure mechanism. A defendant may try to show that an uncounseled plea reflected expedience or inability to contest the accusation rather than reliable proof of conduct. The arithmetic point is not optional, departure is not mandatory, and an actual departure is not assumed. Nichols did not seek departure on that ground; the Court does not decide whether such a showing could be made. Souter does not create a general right to relitigate every conviction, expand Custis's ACCA forum, or use a hypothetical departure as harmlessness for an otherwise unconstitutional score. He reserves a system that makes the conviction conclusive and automatically requires greater incarceration. Because Blackmun's original-invalidity position did not address Baldasar's assumed-valid premise, Souter finds no single governing rationale there on that question; he does not join general overruling.

Blackmun dissents, joined by Stevens and Ginsburg. Even accepting the earlier fine conviction as valid under Scott, they would prevent it from supplying the legal basis for increased incarceration without counsel's reliability protection. Counsel at the new prosecution cannot recreate the assistance missing when the prior accusation was tested. This is not a theory of double punishment: independently established conduct in a counseled, lawful sentencing procedure raises a different question from automatic legal weight assigned to the prior judgment. Argersinger, Burgett and Tucker supply the liberty and reliability concerns, while Lewis's conviction-status disability is distinct. They regard discretionary departure as an inadequate substitute for lost counsel, particularly in ordinary rather than unusual cases. They would reverse the stated use ground and remand with the Government's waiver and indigency alternatives still to be considered, without vacating the Georgia fine or ordering a fixed sentence reduction.

Blackmun additionally retains his own Scott dissent's view that appointed counsel is required for an indigent accused when more than six months' imprisonment is authorized, or when imprisonment is actually imposed, absent valid waiver. The DUI's one-year exposure bears on that personal rule. It is not Scott's holding. His Baldasar concurrence rested on that original-invalidity premise, while he now also joins the later-use reliability objection. Ginsburg separately explains that her Custis majority join concerns a review forum, whereas this case concerns the use of a conviction accepted as valid; the positions are consistent.

Stone-Zsela separately dissents. He would vacate the sentencing judgment and remand to resolve the preserved waiver issue under the proper law and burden; absent counsel or valid waiver, he would exclude the automatic uncounseled-conviction point. He would not order removal while waiver remains unresolved. His proposed protection extends Baldasar's liberty concern to this Guidelines increment; he does not claim it was already a five-Justice rule. The rise from a 210-month ceiling to a 235-month sentence is real confinement, although the statutory maximum remains life. Independent past conduct may receive separately authorized, reliably supported consideration with a genuine opportunity to contest it, but the same conviction cannot simply be relabeled as independent proof. He preserves the original fine, requires no new universal proof standard, and promises no release or particular sentence. He does not decide a general future-warning duty. On the Government's distinct indigency contention he joins the Court's nonresolution and adds no ground; his expressed waiver-remand position remains unchanged.

### 1994-06-06 — [Department of Revenue of Montana v. Kurth Ranch; No. 93-144.](../records/Department_of_Revenue_of_Montana_v_Kurth_Ranch_merits_1994-06-06.md#public-projection)

Justice O'Connor would vacate and remand. She accepts scrutiny of a tax under Halper but requires the challenger first to establish a sanction lacking a rational relationship to legitimate nonpunitive costs or objectives. Only then must the government justify the amount through accounting. The lower courts' reliance on Montana's failure to offer cost evidence cannot replace that initial inquiry. Montana's opportunity to present evidence does not itself relieve the Kurths of the threshold showing. She would have the courts examine the proper inquiry on the existing record and any lawful further proceedings, without treating unproved statewide cost estimates as findings. She does not find the levy finally valid or join an unconditional reversal.

Justice Scalia, joined by Justice Thomas, would reverse on a different ground. The Double Jeopardy Clause, in their view, prohibits successive criminal proceedings; it does not independently prohibit legislatively authorized multiple punishments imposed through a civil proceeding. They would abandon Halper's contrary rule, acknowledging Scalia's earlier join in that decision. Legislative authorization, due process and other constitutional limits remain distinct. Under Ward, civil design may be overcome only by the clearest proof of criminal character, which they do not find here. They do not join O'Connor's conditional accounting remand, and their position does not restore the state-law-invalid portions of the claim. Their proposal does not alter the Court's retained Halper rule.

### 1994-06-06 — [Digital Equipment Corp. v. Desktop Direct, Inc.; No. 93-405.](../records/Digital_Equipment_Corp_v_Desktop_Direct_merits_1994-06-06.md#public-projection)

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela joins Part I and the judgment. He emphasizes that a settlement can purchase peace that later review cannot perfectly restore: renewed litigation may impose irretrievable expense and disclosure. The mere possibility of review is not a complete answer. His ground is that Congress's finality rule tolerates those losses for this contractual category, which lacks the importance needed for the narrow exception. Contract wording and a party's assertion that rescission was plainly wrong cannot create jurisdiction. He preserves final review, independently available statutory procedures and ordinary lawful protections without adopting Part II's separate adequate-vindication explanation as an independently sufficient ground. He does not decide that any particular remedy is available or that every private right must await judgment.

### 1994-06-06 — [Farmer v. Brennan; No. 92-7247.](../records/Farmer_v_Brennan_merits_1994-06-06.md#public-projection)

Justice Blackmun joins the Court because it applies the unchallenged deliberate-indifference framework while preserving workable circumstantial proof and prevention of future harm. He would overrule Wilson's general subjective requirement: objectively inhumane conditions may violate the Eighth Amendment without identifying an official with the required state of mind. That broader position does not change the rule the Court adopts here.

Justice Stevens joins the Court's application but maintains that confinement conditions imposed on a prisoner can constitute punishment without improper subjective motivation. His disagreement with Wilson does not impose a purposeful-malice requirement or prevent his join in the present actual-awareness rule.

Justice Thomas concurs only in the judgment. In his view, not every condition or unauthorized omission is punishment within the Eighth Amendment. Because the parties do not seek overruling Estelle or Wilson, he accepts a cautionary remand within their framework to determine whether Farmer's silence was treated as dispositive. The remand does not declare the officials liable or prevent renewed summary judgment after the proper inquiry. His judgment vote does not join the Court's broader constitutional or prospective-relief explanation.

### 1994-06-06 — [Key Tronic Corp. v. United States; No. 93-376.](../records/Key_Tronic_Corp_v_United_States_merits_1994-06-06.md#public-projection)

Justice Scalia, joined by Justices Blackmun and Thomas, concurs in Parts II and III and dissents from Part I. They accept the American Rule but find Congress's exception in the enacted terms: §101(25) expressly includes related enforcement activities in response, and a qualifying action enforces §107(a)(4)(B)'s payment obligation. Necessary legal expenses of that action therefore come within the costs the statute authorizes. No incantation of the words attorney's fees is required, and express fee provisions in other settings do not narrow the definition they consider clear. Their position does not rest on a free-standing policy of rewarding private enforcement. Necessity, relation to response, consistency with the national contingency plan, allocation and proof of amount still govern; they would remand those matters rather than order an automatic award. They agree that identifying the Air Force independently furthered response and that negotiating Key Tronic's own liability did not enforce another party's obligation. Their partial joins do not adopt the Court's exclusion of private litigation fees.

### 1994-06-13 — [City of Ladue v. Gilleo, No. 92-1856; October Term 1993.](../records/City_of_Ladue_v_Gilleo_merits_1994-06-13.md#public-projection)

O'Connor concurs in the entire Court opinion and judgment. Ordinarily a court first determines whether regulation is content based and then applies the corresponding level of scrutiny. Here the City loses even under the content-neutral assumption, so the Court may decide the residential-channel defect without resolving each exemption. That unusual analytical economy does not create a general license to bypass content classification or diminish the usual protection against content discrimination. Her concurrence supplies no additional controlling holding.

Stone-Zsela concurs in the entire Court opinion and judgment. Removing exemptions would suppress additional signs without restoring the lost opportunity to express one's views at home. Deletion alone therefore cannot cure the residential-channel defect, and otherwise lawful exempt expression should not be eliminated as the supposed remedy. He leaves genuinely tailored physical regulation available. This qualification does not resolve a formal severability question or approve any new ordinance; it supplies no additional Court holding.

### 1994-06-13 — [Department of Taxation and Finance of New York v. Milhelm Attea & Bros., Inc., No. 93-377; 512 U.S. 61.](../records/Department_of_Taxation_and_Finance_of_New_York_v_Milhelm_Attea_and_Bros_Inc_merits_1994-06-13.md#public-projection)

The Court issues no separate writing in this case. No noncontrolling proposition is added for later modeling.

### 1994-06-13 — [Howlett v. Birkdale Shipping Co., S.A., No. 93-670; 512 U.S. 92.](../records/Howlett_v_Birkdale_Shipping_Co_SA_merits_1994-06-13.md#public-projection)

The Court issues no separate writing in this case. No noncontrolling proposition is added for later modeling.

### 1994-06-13 — [Ibanez v. Florida Department of Business and Professional Regulation, Board of Accountancy, No. 93-639; October Term 1993.](../records/Ibanez_v_Florida_Board_of_Accountancy_merits_1994-06-13.md#public-projection)

O'Connor concurs in Part I and the CPA disposition and dissents from Parts II and III and reversal of the CFP ground. Her Peel dissent distinguishes literal truth from what prospective clients reasonably understand. The unexplained CFP initials appear beside a state CPA credential without naming a certifier whose standards the consumer can verify. She regards that specific presentation as inherently misleading about governmental recognition and would sustain paragraph (i)'s prohibition as applied, in the context of the Board's specialty-recognition charge under (g). She does not find a different, uncharged violation or declare every truthful statement about private certification inherently deceptive. The active, state-issued CPA title lacks that source ambiguity, so she joins its protection.

She independently concludes that, even if the CFP advertisement is only potentially misleading, paragraph (j)'s actual disclosure application is valid here. Zauderer allows responsive factual clarification, and the advertisement leaves both governmental status and recognition standards unexplained. She accepts the immediate-proximity statement that the recognizing agency is neither affiliated with nor sanctioned by state or federal government, plus its recognition requirements including but not limited to education, experience and testing, as responsive to those ambiguities in this application. This is approval of the actual burden in this case, not a substitute hypothetical one-line rule or a universal endorsement of every application. Her strongest contrary consideration is that useful factual information may be suppressed by extensive disclosure; she concludes the present unexplained certification can lawfully require the demanded explanation. Regulatory experience and the advertisement itself suffice for her inference without a proved deceived client. Compliance with (j) does not become an exception to the independent (i) prohibition. She would reverse the CPA basis, preserve the CFP basis and permit conforming administrative treatment if the sanction needs separation, without dictating a future penalty. Both grounds are noncontrolling.

### 1994-06-13 — [Livadas v. Bradshaw, No. 92-1920; 512 U.S. 107.](../records/Livadas_v_Bradshaw_merits_1994-06-13.md#public-projection)

The Court issues no separate writing in this case. No noncontrolling proposition is added for later modeling.

### 1994-06-13 — [O’Melveny & Myers v. FDIC, No. 93-489; October Term 1993.](../records/OMelveny_Myers_v_FDIC_merits_1994-06-13.md#public-projection)

Stevens concurs, joined by Blackmun, O'Connor and Souter, while all four join the entire Court opinion. Federal judicial restraint does not require California's common law to remain fixed or dictate imputation against the receiver. Erie distinguishes a federal court's creation of a new national rule from legitimate state common-law development. State courts may adapt their own rules to the displacement of fraudulent insiders and protection of innocent creditors within their lawmaking authority; a federal court adjudicating these claims must ascertain California law rather than invent a federal substitute. The availability and content of any California receiver-protective rule remain unresolved. This explanation does not establish a new federal exception, direct California to excuse imputation, or find malpractice proved. Its state-law-development emphasis is a noncontrolling concurrence, not an alternative Court holding.

### 1994-06-13 — [Romano v. Oklahoma, No. 92-9093; 512 U.S. 1 (1994).](../records/Romano_v_Oklahoma_merits_1994-06-13.md#public-projection)

Justice Blackmun concurs in the case-specific responsibility holding and accepts the plurality's new-hearing relief as a minimum, while independently concluding that capital punishment as presently administered cannot provide the consistency, individualized fairness and reliability required by the Eighth Amendment. His objection remains even if the challenged disclosure is removed. The guided-sentencing structure sustained in Gregg and Jurek seeks to reconcile restraint of arbitrary punishment with individual consideration; in his view, narrowing eligibility and permitting full mitigation still leave arbitrary selection among eligible offenders. Lockett and Eddings protect individual mitigating consideration, and Graham and Johnson v. Texas enforce that protection. He joined the latter decisions; they are not examples of the Court refusing effective mitigation. He nevertheless considers that protection insufficient to answer the separate selection problem. His position leaves theoretically open procedures that truly provide consistency, fairness and reliability, although he doubts they can be achieved. He would vacate death and require a lawful noncapital disposition through state proceedings, without selecting a particular punishment, acquittal or release. He makes no new finding about Romano's innocence, race, counsel or discriminatory treatment. This position is his alone; it neither overrules Gregg or Jurek nor turns a general challenge to capital punishment into a party claim within the limited question presented.

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela concurs in the responsibility holding and limited appellate vacatur/remand. Under Chapman, he would first require the State to prove beyond a reasonable doubt that the unconstitutional disclosure did not contribute to the selection of death. Eligibility for death does not establish harmlessness concerning its selection: neither the four findings originally made by the jury nor the three aggravators remaining after appellate reweighing answers that question. Conversely, the constitutional violation does not by itself establish that the error is structural and immune from harmless-error review. The original reweighing did not apply the new responsibility rule, and the Court should not make a conclusive prejudice determination without the required record-based inquiry. If the State fails to establish constitutional harmlessness and continues to seek death, he would require a new penalty hearing without the irrelevant separate death judgment. Independently admissible prior-conduct evidence and the guilt adjudication remain available and untouched, respectively. His responsibility rule considers the prominence and prosecutorial use of the information, its avoidable inclusion in proof of conviction, and any specific corrective instruction; it does not require exclusion of relevant conduct or declare that no focused instruction could ever suffice. He does not adopt ordinary due process as an alternative sufficient ground. His proposed harmlessness route has no other join and is not the Court's further-remedy instruction.

Justice O'Connor dissents, joined by Justices Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas. Caldwell, as explained by Darden, Dugger and Sawyer, requires misleading information about the jury's legal responsibility. The exhibit was true when admitted, appeal information made its nonfinality explicit, and the instructions accurately assigned the present jury its own choice. The possibility that another death sentence influenced deliberations does not establish the required misdescription. The four separately reject ordinary due-process relief because the entire proceeding does not cross Donnelly and Darden's fundamental-unfairness threshold. Estelle preserves the distinction between state-law irrelevance and federal constitutional error. Truth is not categorical immunity from all other constitutional objections, and correct general instructions are not an automatic cure in every case. But this record does not establish either asserted violation for these Justices. Woodson and Lockett protect individualized sentencing rather than a federal evidence code. Zant's concern with aggravation based on constitutionally protected conduct is not the present objection; Johnson v. Mississippi's invalid-predicate concern is distinct because the prior-violent-felony aggravator was removed and three others reweighed. That decision differs from Johnson v. Texas's mitigation holding. The dissent does not endorse the irrelevant exhibit, restore the deleted aggravator, use later Thompson proceedings as a cure, or treat eligibility as harmlessness. Its no-error conclusion makes a conditional harmless-error position unnecessary.

Souter's plurality, joined by Blackmun, Stevens and Ginsburg, requires a new sentencing hearing if the State continues to seek death. They cannot exclude the effect of the official introduction of the other death judgment on the jury's overall responsibility; removing an aggravator and reweighing does not remove that concern. They do not find an identified juror's actual state of mind and do not adopt a general rule that every evidentiary capital error is structural. They retain the new-hearing requirement on this record rather than authorize the proposed first harmlessness inquiry. Guilt and independently admissible underlying conduct remain untouched. Blackmun's broader noncapital preference remains separate from his minimum case-specific join.

### 1994-06-13 — [United States v. Carlton, No. 92-1941; 512 U.S. 26.](../records/United_States_v_Carlton_merits_1994-06-13.md#public-projection)

Justice O'Connor concurs in the judgment. Legitimate revenue collection and the permissible short period sustain this amendment, but she does not make speculation about the earlier Congress's intent the foundation for constitutional validity. Duration independently matters. Taxpayers ordinarily face some limited retrospective adjustment, and this application falls within that allowance despite real reliance. Her concern that a period extending farther than the year preceding the legislative session would raise serious constitutional questions is a reservation about another case, not a categorical one-year invalidity rule. She accepts the express statutory reach but does not join the Court's complete corrective-purpose explanation.

Justice Scalia, joined by Justice Thomas, concurs in the judgment. Following their position in TXO Production Corp. v. Alliance Resources Corp., they reject the asserted substantive economic protection under the Due Process Clause; no sufficient procedural defect is presented. They therefore accept the express amendment and its consequence without joining the Court's rational-purpose and modest-period method. On the premise that the Clause supplies the substantive protection claimed, the Government's defeat of deliberate reliance would trouble them: describing the interval as short does not undo that injury. Their judgment votes are not additional support for the Court's constitutional method. They would reverse and remand on their distinct due-process ground, without an allocation finding or a compensatory award.

## 5. Current Procedure and Institution

Stone is Chief Justice. The Associate Justices in seniority order are Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg. All nine seats are occupied; Ginsburg took the judicial oath on August 10, 1993 and is seated throughout the Term. Six Justices constitute a quorum. A majority of participating Justices controls a judgment; a referred application uses the participating-majority rule. Thomas and Ginsburg do not participate in No. 93-5252 (*Sassower v. Reno*); no other case-specific nonparticipation is established at opening, and each event validates participation at argument or submission and again at decision.

Stone assigns an opinion whenever he is in the judgment majority. Otherwise the most senior participating Associate in that majority assigns, in the order Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg. An equally divided Court affirms without precedential effect.

## Circuit allotments

Under the order effective August 10, 1993, governing through August 2, 1994: Stone, District of Columbia, Fourth, and Federal; Souter, First; Ginsburg, Second; Scalia, Third and Fifth; Stevens, Sixth and Seventh; Blackmun, Eighth; Kennedy, Ninth; O'Connor, Tenth; Thomas, Eleventh. No reallotment is scheduled within the Term.

## Standing practices

Referral of applications, effective October 7, 1991: an application for a stay, injunction, or other interim relief in a matter on the Term's inventory is referred by the Circuit Justice to the full Court and decided by the participating Justices under the participating-majority rule. The order issues in the Court's name and recites the presenting Justice; it ordinarily issues without opinion, and a brief per curiam and noted dissents are available. A Justice may participate in a referred application from the date of the judicial oath. The three Rule 39 filing-relief matters in the inventory (*Day v. Day* and *In re Sassower*, October 12, 1993; *In re Anderson*, May 2, 1994) follow this practice; Stone presents the Day and Sassower groups by designation and Anderson under the District of Columbia allotment.

Case-specific completed participation: Blackmun did not participate in Irvine; O’Connor did not participate in Morgan Stanley. No reason is supplied and neither absence is a standing recusal for another matter.

- **Carried open from Standing State, none scheduled:** *Zatko v. California* (fee motions denied November 4, 1991; underlying petitions unresolved); *Wyoming v. Oklahoma*, No. 112, Original (original jurisdiction retained after the January 22, 1992 declaration and injunction); *Reynolds v. International Amateur Athletic Federation*, No. A-954 (limited interim protection pending further order); *Grubbs v. Delo*, No. A-324 (application pending; temporary administrative stay pending further order); *United States v. Louisiana*, No. 9, Original (supplemental decree of February 22, 1993; jurisdiction retained); *Delaware v. New York*, No. 111, Original (remanded to the Special Master March 30, 1993); *Nebraska v. Wyoming*, No. 108, Original (partial summary judgments April 20, 1993; proceedings retained before the Special Master). Each awaits a verified dated event.
- **Inventory:** 95 supplied Court events, October 12, 1993 through June 30, 1994, in eight chunks; three are Rule 39 applications and ninety-two are merits matters, including per curiam procedural and summary dispositions. Event dates are the inventory's historical opinion or order dates, used for ordering only.
- **Expressly sequenced same-day pairs:** *Day* then *Sassower* (October 12, 1993); *McDermott v. AmClyde* then *Boca Grande* (April 20, 1994, across the chunk 3/4 boundary); *Landgraf* then *Rivers* (April 26, 1994); *Staples* then *Posters 'N' Things* (May 23, 1994); *Holder v. Hall* then *Johnson v. De Grandy* (June 30, 1994). Every other same-day group is uncoordinated and shares its start-of-day entering-law baseline.
- **Cross-date dependencies** enter by effective date; only an admitted effective Record changes entering law.
- **Institutional calendar:** no roster, allotment, or practice change within the Term. Breyer's accession on August 3, 1994 falls after the last inventory event.

### Procedure resulting from completed events

- **1993-10-12 — Day v. Day, Nos. 92-8788, 92-8792, 92-8888, 92-8905, 92-8906, 92-9018, 92-9101, and 93-5430.** No new prospective fee-and-form condition is imposed. The current Rule 39 motions in Nos. 92-8788, 92-8792, 92-8888, 92-8905, 92-8906, 92-9018, 92-9101, and 93-5430 remain unadjudicated by this decision. That statement describes the decision's scope: it grants or denies no current motion, enters no stay or continuance, fixes no payment or correction deadline, and directs no new filing or further-consideration procedure. No petition for certiorari or underlying claim is disposed of. Existing rules remain operative. No current-fee component is carried forward.
- **1993-10-12 — In re Sassower, Nos. 92-8933, 92-8934, 92-9228, 93-5045, 93-5127, 93-5128, 93-5129, 93-5252, 93-5358, and 93-5596.** Neither proposed new prospective condition is imposed. The current fee motions in Nos. 92-8933, 92-8934, 92-9228, 93-5045, 93-5127, 93-5128, 93-5129, 93-5252, 93-5358, and 93-5596 remain unadjudicated by this decision, including No. 93-5252 with its distinct participation. No current fee motion, certiorari petition, or extraordinary writ is granted or denied. The Court orders no stay, continuance, payment or correction deadline, additional submission, new clerk screen, or further-consideration procedure. Existing rules and restrictions are not invalidated or vacated. No underlying judgment is affirmed or reversed. No current-fee component is carried forward.
- **1993-11-09 — Harris v. Forklift Systems, Inc., No. 92-1168.** The Sixth Circuit's judgment is reversed and the case is remanded for further proceedings consistent with the stated standard, including reconsideration of the existing record and any proceedings the trial court lawfully requires. No liability finding, damages award, or constructive-discharge finding is directed; those matters are not resolved by this disposition.
- **1993-11-09 — Florence County School District Four v. Carter, No. 91-1523.** The Fourth Circuit's judgment affirming reimbursement is affirmed. The reimbursement judgment remains in force; the district court's ordinary equitable authority over a reasonable amount is preserved. The Court does not reopen the settled education findings, order a new merits remand, require state approval of Trident, or order general financing of private education.
- **1993-11-30 — Cavanaugh v. Roller, No. 92-1510.** The Fourth Circuit's judgment is affirmed. Its remand for a declaration restoring Roller's annual parole reconsideration under the pre-amendment rule remains in force; the district court may issue an injunction if necessary to secure that consideration. An injunction is not mandatory merely because the constitutional claim succeeds. The Board retains authority to deny parole under lawful standards, and no release, damages award, immunity reconsideration, contempt finding, or specific hearing date is ordered. The official- and individual-capacity damages dismissals remain undisturbed. Ordinary implementation may account for actual compliance and legally effective later developments, including the Act's savings terms, without assuming those events have already occurred. This Supreme Court review is complete; no further hearing-status report, hold, or return condition is imposed by this decision.
- **1993-11-30 — Izumi Seimitsu Kogyo Kabushiki Kaisha v. U.S. Philips Corp., No. 92-1123.** The writ is dismissed as improvidently granted. The Federal Circuit's denial of intervention and its vacatur orders remain undisturbed, including the underlying direction to dismiss the settled case with prejudice. This Court issues no new remand, does not reinstate a district-court judgment, and does not decide collateral estoppel in the related litigation. Proceedings on this writ are concluded subject to ordinarily available post-decision procedures.
- **1993-12-13 — United States v. James Daniel Good Real Property, No. 92-1180** The case returns to the lower courts for proceedings consistent with the two component rulings. The timely forfeiture claim remains pending; lawful consequences of the unconstitutional interim seizure require their own remedial basis. No date for further proceedings is fixed.
- **1993-12-13 — John Hancock Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Harris Trust & Savings Bank, No. 92-1074** The case returns for further district-court proceedings on the fiduciary claims under the affirmed judgment. Fiduciary status does not establish breach, causation, loss, or a monetary or equitable award. No further hearing date is fixed.
- **1993-12-13 — Tennessee v. Middlebrooks; No. 92-989.** Supreme Court review ends. Further proceedings follow the Tennessee Supreme Court's existing direction for a new sentencing hearing. The lower court found the torture aggravator supported but could not conclude that removing the invalid felony-murder aggravator was harmless beyond a reasonable doubt. This Court does not reconsider that finding or import Howell's different harmlessness result. The State remains free to seek death again on a lawful basis; the affirmed convictions remain in force.
- **1994-01-01 — South Carolina 1993 Act No. 184, H.3151; relevant to Cavanaugh v. Roller, No. 92-1510.** This source does not decide Roller's receipt of a hearing or the status of his litigation. It supplies no Court order or change to a lower-court judgment.
- **1994-01-10 — Burden v. Zant; No. 92-8836.** The Supreme Court review event is complete. The case returns to the Eleventh Circuit, which may direct the district court to make the required determinations. The conviction and sentence are not vacated by an immediate habeas writ in this disposition.
- **1994-01-11 — Ratzlaf v. United States; No. 92-1196.** The Supreme Court merits review is complete. Both petitioners' case returns to the Ninth Circuit for proceedings consistent with the required knowledge element and for count-specific implementation. Lawful retrial and properly presented remedial issues remain available; an instructional omission alone supplies no acquittal order.
- **1994-01-19 — Thunder Basin Coal Co. v. Reich; No. 92-896.** The Supreme Court review event is complete. The Tenth Circuit's judgment stands, the district-court injunction remains unavailable, and the operator must use the authorized statutory process for its enforcement-related challenge. This judgment neither resolves the labor merits nor assesses any penalty; it orders no new administrative outcome.
- **1994-01-19 — Weiss v. United States; No. 92-1482, including Hernandez under Rule 12.2.** Both military appellate judgments stand. No remand, appointment proceeding, tenure rule or new trial is directed. Neither this decision nor the guilty pleas foreclose distinct claims beyond the institutional objections decided.
- **1994-01-19 — Schiro v. Farley; No. 92-7549.** The judgment denying these habeas grounds stands. There is no Supreme Court remand and no newly retained proceeding. Other claims, if any, receive no disposition here.
- **1994-01-24 — Albright v. Oliver; No. 92-833.** Supreme Court merits review is complete. The Seventh Circuit's dismissal stands; the Court orders no new trial, damages award or merits remand. Any different claim remains subject to ordinary procedural rules. The state-law count's prior dismissal without prejudice remains unchanged.
- **1994-01-24 — National Organization for Women, Inc. v. Scheidler; No. 92-780.** The case returns for further proceedings on the two clinics' affected §1962(c) and dependent §1962(d) claims under the stated construction. The lower courts must address the remaining statutory elements, each defendant's responsibility, any properly presented First Amendment defense and the lawful basis of each requested remedy. Allegations remain unproved. The Court fixes no next hearing date and retains no separate proceeding.
- **1994-01-24 — ABF Freight System, Inc. v. NLRB; No. 92-1550.** The Tenth Circuit enforcement judgment stands, and the Board may enforce reinstatement and backpay. No remand, recalculation or independent perjury sanction is ordered. The limited grant does not reopen the original reason for discharge.
- **1994-01-24 — Department of Revenue of Oregon v. ACF Industries, Inc.; No. 92-74.** The case returns to the lower courts for proceedings consistent with the Court's construction. The Ninth Circuit's total-exemption requirement is removed. The Court directs no new tax amount and does not reopen an unasserted assessment or rate claim. Any independently preserved claim remains governed by its ordinary procedural posture; the targeted-tax reservation creates no new finding or entitlement to relief.
- **1994-01-24 — Northwest Airlines, Inc. v. County of Kent; No. 92-97.** The Sixth Circuit's judgment is affirmed. The District Court's unappealed parking recalculation and the Sixth Circuit's remand to allocate crash, fire and rescue costs between airlines and general aviation remain operative. The Court orders no additional fee recalculation, refund, deadline or separate constitutional remand. The existing cost-allocation proceedings continue under the surviving lower-court rulings; the outcome of a later lawful Transportation Department proceeding or regulation is not prejudged.
- **1994-02-23 — Hagen v. Utah; No. 92-6281.** The conviction reinstated by the Utah Supreme Court remains in place. No merits remand, title adjudication, damages award or order reallocating all jurisdiction in the region issues. Proceedings implementing the affirmed judgment remain for the Utah courts.
- **1994-02-23 — FDIC v. Meyer; No. 92-741.** The agency damages judgment is reversed because the asserted direct constitutional cause is unavailable. Ordinary proceedings implementing reversal must reject that agency cause; no new evidentiary inquiry or damages trial is directed. Pattullo's judgment remains undisturbed, and the Court does not adjudicate the due-process merits or award another remedy.
- **1994-02-23 — Department of Defense v. Federal Labor Relations Authority; No. 92-1223.** Supreme Court merits review is complete. The Fifth Circuit's enforcement judgment is reversed as to both FLRA orders, eliminating compelled disclosure on the asserted §552a(b)(2) basis. Ordinary lower-court implementation follows that ruling. No new hearing on necessity, employee motives or harassment is directed, and no order requires creation or approval of a routine use. A different disclosure route must satisfy its own requirements in a properly presented proceeding.
- **1994-02-23 — Elder v. Holloway; No. 92-8579.** The Court of Appeals must reconsider qualified immunity independently using all relevant authority and the proper summary-judgment record. The officers may prevail or further proceedings may be necessary under that inquiry; neither result is directed. Ultimate Fourth Amendment liability, exigent circumstances, damages and immunity remain undecided.
- **1994-02-23 — Caspari v. Bohlen; No. 92-1500.** The Eighth Circuit's conditional-habeas direction on this claim is reversed. Proceedings implementing the mandate must respect the nonretroactivity bar and the reserved merits. The Court directs no new evidentiary hearing, State proof opportunity, release deadline or replacement sentence and leaves the robbery convictions otherwise undisturbed.
- **1994-02-23 — American Dredging Co. v. Miller; No. 91-1950.** The Louisiana Supreme Court's reversal and remand stand. The state trial court may proceed on the Jones Act and general maritime claims; negligence, unseaworthiness, wages, maintenance, cure, and damages remain unadjudicated. The Court orders no interstate transfer, convenience reweighing, or liability award. The employer's unpursued personal-jurisdiction objection is not decided.
- **1994-03-01 — Fogerty v. Fantasy, Inc.; No. 92-1750.** The fee application returns for reconsideration under the correct standard. The lower-court finding of neither frivolousness nor bad faith is not converted into a contrary finding; it simply ceases to be a categorical barrier. A reasonable award, an appropriately limited amount, or a reasoned denial remains available under the governing statute. No fee is directed, no infringement issue is retried, and the unrelated counterclaim rulings remain undisturbed.
- **1994-03-07 — Campbell v. Acuff-Rose Music, Inc.; No. 92-1292.** The case returns for proceedings consistent with all four statutory factors. The extent of justified musical borrowing and evidence of a legitimate nonparody rap-derivative market require further consideration. The Court does not direct a final fair-use or infringement judgment, an award, a compulsory license, or an injunction. The unappealed state-claim preemption determination and denial of leave to add a Lanham Act claim remain outside review. The initial release/request sequence is disputed; no bad faith is found from it, and the sampling allegation is not adjudicated.
- **1994-03-07 — Liteky v. United States; No. 92-6921.** Supreme Court merits review is complete. The convictions remain undisturbed on the recusal issue, and the Court orders no retrial, reassignment or additional recusal procedure. No separate Supreme Court matter is retained.
- **1994-03-22 — United States v. Granderson, No. 92-1662.** Supreme Court review is complete. The Eleventh Circuit's vacatur of the twenty-month term and release order remain operative, and revocation remains intact. The Court orders no new sentencing proceeding or substitute sanction; the original fine's payment status and unpresented monetary implementation are undecided.
- **1994-03-22 — Victor v. Nebraska, No. 92-8894; Sandoval v. California, No. 92-9049.** Supreme Court review of the two instruction questions is complete. The Nebraska denial of postconviction relief and California's judgment remain undisturbed on those questions. No new trial, release or additional sentencing proceeding is ordered by the Court.
- **1994-03-30 — Powell v. Nevada, No. 92-8841.** The case returns to the Nevada Supreme Court for further proceedings consistent with application of McLaughlin and consideration of properly available remaining grounds. The court must first consider the timing and any legally sufficient justification, then the remaining remedial issues as necessary. The vacatur itself orders no suppression, retrial, release, damages, acquittal or ultimate reversal of the conviction.
- **1994-04-04 — Oregon Waste Systems, Inc. v. Department of Environmental Quality, No. 93-70; Columbia Resource Co. v. Environmental Quality Commission, No. 93-108.** The matters return to the Oregon Supreme Court for proceedings consistent with invalidity of the challenged origin differential. Relief must operate through the existing review proceeding. The Court does not set a new rate, decide refund entitlement or amount, alter the separately unchallenged charge, compel particular shipments or restore the legislative-committee approval provisions severed under state law.
- **1994-04-04 — Ticor Title Insurance Co. v. Brown; No. 92-1988.** Proceedings on this writ are complete. The Ninth Circuit's injunction-preclusion affirmance and monetary-preclusion reversal and remand remain undisturbed, with further proceedings under its existing mandate. This Court orders no new remand, damages award, settlement modification, vacatur or mootness dismissal. The pending proposal remains subject to District Court approval and its legally operative terms; no later outcome or hearing date is fixed.
- **1994-04-19 — J.E.B. v. Alabama ex rel. T.B.; No. 92-1239.** The case returns to the Alabama courts for the proper jury-selection inquiry and the conditional new-trial remedy. Independent evidence issues and paternity remain for their proper proceedings. There is no retained Supreme Court proceeding, prescribed quota or automatic finding about an explanation the trial court has not assessed.
- **1994-04-19 — Central Bank of Denver, N.A. v. First Interstate Bank of Denver, N.A.; No. 92-854; 511 U.S. 164 (1994).** The case returns through the Tenth Circuit for further proceedings on the reinstated private affirmative-assistance claim consistent with the Court’s standard. The existing summary judgment cannot stand on the rejected categorical grounds or on the disputed culpability and assistance record. The purchasers retain the ordinary civil burden on every required element; the bank may contest fraud, awareness, assisting effect, reliance, causation and loss. No finding of ultimate liability, damages award, automatic indemnity, liability of another defendant or unpleaded primary claim is entered. Independently preserved claims and defenses retain their own rules. The Court retains no separate proceeding and fixes no later hearing or outcome.
- **1994-04-20 — McDermott, Inc. v. AmClyde; No. 92-1479.** The matter returns through the Fifth Circuit for proceedings consistent with proportionate credit, including any properly remaining evidentiary question. The Court retains no separate proceeding and orders no unconditional payment. Any distinct contribution action must be addressed in its own procedural posture.
- **1994-04-20 — Boca Grande Club, Inc. v. Florida Power & Light Co., No. 93-180.** The Eleventh Circuit judgment is vacated, and the case is remanded for further proceedings consistent with McDermott and the contribution rule stated here. The Court does not reinstate every district-court rationale or calculate the settlement credit. Boca Grande’s alternative factual contention that Florida Power & Light has no contribution entitlement was not decided below and is not independently resolved here. No allocation, damages award, exoneration or limitation judgment is entered by this disposition.
- **1994-04-20 — United States v. Irvine, No. 92-1546; 511 U.S. 224.** The Eighth Circuit judgment is reversed. Ordinary implementation of that reversal must conform to taxation of the 1979 disposition and rejection of the asserted refund ground. The Court orders no special remand for hypothetical valuation disputes, sets no new payment amount, and does not reassess unrelated items or decide the 1980 assessment.
- **1994-04-26 — Landgraf v. USI Film Products, No. 92-757.** The Fifth Circuit judgment is affirmed. No new damages trial, damages award or remand is ordered. The judgment on the earlier equitable-remedy record remains intact, including the unreviewed constructive-discharge findings. The Court neither declares the harassment lawful nor decides the merits of a different claim for relief available under prior law.
- **1994-04-26 — Rivers v. Roadway Express, Inc., No. 92-938.** The Sixth Circuit judgment is affirmed and the case remanded for proceedings consistent with the temporal ruling. Its existing contract-enforcement jury remand continues. The Court neither dismisses that claim nor determines discriminatory motive, damages or the scope of retrial beyond the reviewed temporal question. The separate Title VII result and the unreviewed labor-contract, union and Harvis matters are not reopened.
- **1994-04-26 — Stansbury v. California, No. 93-5770.** The case returns to the California Supreme Court to reconsider custody without the erroneous subjective-focus factor and to address any properly reached consequences. Reversal does not itself order suppression, a new trial, acquittal, release or resentencing. The Court does not decide the validity of the death penalty or supply a separate disposition of the sentence.
- **1994-05-02 — City of Chicago v. Environmental Defense Fund, No. 92-1639.** The Seventh Circuit judgment is affirmed. Exemption-based summary judgment for Chicago remains unavailable, and the citizen action continues for the appropriate coverage, procedural and remedial determinations. The Court does not prescribe a disposal technology, injunction, penalty, damages award or cleanup allocation; any requested enforcement relief must satisfy its own requirements, including any applicable notice limits. No finding that every ash sample is hazardous or that every possible on-site permit is required follows from this decision.
- **1994-05-02 — United States v. Alvarez-Sanchez, No. 92-1812.** The Ninth Circuit judgment is reversed and the case remanded for proceedings consistent with the narrow statutory rule. The Court does not itself direct admission of the confession, reinstate the conviction, order a new trial, suppress evidence, grant acquittal or release, or adjudicate the unpreserved Gerstein/McLaughlin theory. Properly available remaining issues retain their own legal and procedural requirements; no success or waiver on an unidentified claim is invented.
- **1994-05-02 — In re Anderson, No. 93-8312.** The underlying extraordinary-writ petition remains pending for independent consideration. Rule 20 requires the applicable exceptional circumstances and inadequacy of relief through another form or court; §2241 and the applicable habeas requirements remain separate. Financial leave does not establish that D.C. Code §23-110 remedies are inadequate or ineffective, excuse a default, grant a hearing, order a response, vacate a conviction, reopen another docket or extend a deadline. No payment-cure deadline is set because the fee motion is granted. No existing valid restriction is vacated or enlarged.
- **1994-05-16 — C & A Carbone, Inc. v. Town of Clarkstown, No. 92-1402; 511 U.S. 383.** The state appellate judgment is reversed on the challenged Commerce Clause enforcement ground, and the case is remanded for proceedings consistent with this decision, including appropriate adjustment of the injunction. Other appeals dismissed below as superseded or terminated are not revived. Independent valid zoning, health, permitting, dumping, recycling and financing measures remain subject to their own law and the ordinance's severability clause. No damages, refund, compelled acceptance of a particular load, contract rescission, substitute tax or fee, or mandate deadline is fixed.
- **1994-05-16 — Kokkonen v. Guardian Life Insurance Co. of America, No. 93-263; October Term 1993.** The case returns for proceedings implementing dismissal of the enforcement motion for lack of the asserted jurisdiction. The parties remain free to pursue an appropriate state contract action or a new federal action meeting its own jurisdictional requirements. No finding resolves breach, damages, specific performance, entitlement to Rule 60(b) relief, or diversity for a new claim.
- **1994-05-16 — Beecham v. United States and Jones v. United States, No. 93-445, including Jones under Rule 12.2; October Term 1993.** The two Fourth Circuit judgments remain in force. Jones's restored West Virginia convictions remain excluded; the federal predicate supports the appellate reinstatement/remand disposition, not an adjudication of guilt. The linked false-statement count remains in that procedural posture without an independent ruling on its elements. No particular federal pardon, restoration process, §925(c) relief, civil-rights bundle, automatic-restoration rule, or federal-habeas set-aside question is decided. Any separate properly presented ground follows its own requirements.
- **1994-05-16 — Security Services, Inc. v. Kmart Corp., No. 93-284; October Term 1993.** The Third Circuit judgment and Kmart's summary judgment stand. The Court orders no further calculation, substituted tariff, agency referral, or bankruptcy distribution. It does not decide contract carriage, rate reasonableness, estoppel, collateral estoppel, or unpresented statutory relief. The waived limitations defense and unpreserved reasonable-compensation theory are not revived. No election or remedy under the Negotiated Rates Act is adjudicated. Thomas and Ginsburg would remand after rejecting invalidity, leaving other properly presented defenses for further proceedings rather than immediately awarding undercharges.
- **1994-05-23 — BFP v. Resolution Trust Corp.; No. 92-1370; 511 U.S. 531.** The case returns through the Ninth Circuit for further proceedings in both affected lower-court routes. The rejected no-property-interest alternative is not revived. No automatic avoidance, title transfer, refund, fixed recovery or nationwide percentage is ordered. Any remedy must rest on proved avoidance and recovery requirements and applicable transferee protections; final titles outside this timely proceeding are not reopened.
- **1994-05-23 — Staples v. United States; No. 92-1441; 511 U.S. 600.** The case returns through the Tenth Circuit for further proceedings consistent with the correct knowledge requirement. The Court orders no acquittal, fixes no sentence, and neither mandates a retrial nor resolves any properly available sufficiency or error-review issue. The registration law remains in force.
- **1994-05-23 — Custis v. United States; No. 93-5209; 511 U.S. 485.** The sentence remains operative. Custis may pursue legally available review of the state convictions and, following successful invalidation, seek relief through an authorized federal procedure. No stay, automatic reopening, fixed reduced sentence or right to repeated merits review is ordered.
- **1994-05-23 — NLRB v. Health Care & Retirement Corp. of America; No. 92-1964; 511 U.S. 571.** The Sixth Circuit must give effect to the supported employee-status determination and address any independently preserved remaining issues. The Court awards no backpay and does not determine every element of protected conduct or unfair labor practice.
- **1994-05-23 — Dalton v. Specter; No. 93-289; 511 U.S. 462.** Dismiss the claim seeking to block implementation because of the alleged selection defects. Section 2905(c) excludes NEPA from the President's and Commission's actions and from Department of Defense actions except during property disposal and relocation of functions after the receiving installation is selected but before the functions move. In those covered processes, the Secretaries need not consider the need for the selected closure or realignment, the need to transfer functions to the selected receiving installation, or alternative military installations; they may omit those matters but are not required to ignore them. An action seeking review of a NEPA requirement applicable to those covered Defense Department acts or failures to act must be brought no more than 60 days after the challenged act or failure, not 60 days after presidential approval or congressional receipt. No such NEPA claim is adjudicated here. No advisory injunction, merits finding of compliance, or assumed remedy for reserved claims is entered.
- **1994-05-23 — Posters ’N’ Things, Ltd. v. United States; No. 92-903; 511 U.S. 513.** The affirmance leaves the reviewed convictions in place and orders no remand, resentencing or acquittal. No additional merits ruling is made on the separately unpreserved or undeveloped contentions. Properly raised exemption and knowledge challenges remain governed by the complete statutory rule; no issue already resolved in this record is reopened.
- **1994-05-23 — Associated Industries of Missouri v. Lohman; No. 93-397; 511 U.S. 641.** On remand the Missouri courts must apply the local comparison and determine the lawful scope of relief. The availability of meaningful predeprivation review, affected payments, preservation and permissible equalizing remedies remain to be determined. No quantified refund, tax increase, exemption or severability result is ordered, and the unchallenged ordinary taxes remain in force.
- **1994-05-23 — Morgan Stanley & Co. v. Pacific Mutual Life Insurance Co.; No.93-609; 511 U.S.658.** The lower courts must deny the §27A(b) motion in this matter and leave the August 1991 dismissal undisturbed. No remand for fraud findings or determination of damages is ordered.
- **1994-05-23 — McKnight v. General Motors Corp.; No. 92-1113; 511 U.S. 659.** The Seventh Circuit must reconsider counsel's sanction. Its order named attorney John S. Williamson, Jr., and made the $500 payable to the circuit clerk under appellate Rule 38 and Circuit Rule 38. The Court identifies no independent abuse and orders no automatic new sanction or reimbursement. The restored employment dismissal and the separate Rule 60(b)(6) posture remain undisturbed.
- **1994-05-31 — Waters v. Churchill; No.92-1450; 511 U.S.661.** Further proceedings must determine genuine reliance, actual motivation, and the protection of other speech found to have caused discharge, with ordinary summary-judgment and Mt. Healthy rules and preserved personal immunity. The completed investigation's conditional objective adequacy and the reported account's conditional legal permissibility are not reopened as wholly unresolved issues. No damages, reinstatement, municipal policy/custom ruling, revived association claim or final credibility finding is ordered.
- **1994-05-31 — PUD No. 1 of Jefferson County v. Washington Department of Ecology; No. 92-1911; 511 U.S. 700.** The challenged certification condition remains operative in the licensing process. The condition sets seasonal residual flows of 100–200 cubic feet per second, rather than the applicants' proposed 65–155, for the bypassed 1.2-mile reach. The state courts' preservation determination governs the account of state law; this Court supplies no optimal-flow calculation. Applicants must still obtain the separately required water-right permit before construction and a federal license. No FERC license has issued, and no conflicting federal condition is adjudicated. Lawful §401 conditions become license conditions; available review of a later federal license remains. No denial, waiver, notice breach or other unpresented certification-procedure issue is found.
- **1994-06-06 — Nichols v. United States; No.92-8556; 511 U.S.738.** The judgment and sentence remain operative. The Court orders no deduction of the DUI point, vacatur of the Georgia fine, new sentencing hearing, release, change to supervised release, or automatic 25-month reduction. No final ruling on the Government's waiver or indigency alternatives is necessary to the affirmance. The dissents' conditional remand proposals are not the mandate.

Ordinary remands below do not create new retained Supreme Court proceedings. The seven opening carryovers remain open exactly as stated in the manifest; no calendar-based closure, later historical order, or new filing is inferred. The stopped matters or components below also remain open. Day's completed prospective-control refusal enters Sassower in the expressly sequenced same-day order and reinforces the inherited requirement for a supported, tailored restriction. Neither decision grants or denies the current fee motions, creates a categorical prohibition on future controls, or supplies a finding about financial eligibility or particular-petition frivolousness. Anderson received those actual bounded rulings; its separate completed order supplies only the fee and prospective-control consequences stated in its Record.

### Procedure resulting from chunk 6

- **1994-06-06 — Department of Revenue of Montana v. Kurth Ranch; No. 93-144.** The disallowance of the surviving punitive claim remains effective, including the lower judgment's remittance consequence for sums already recovered. Administration and any necessary accounting implement that judgment; the Court fixes no new exact principal, penalty, interest or refund total. The state-law-rejected drug components and the criminal judgments remain undisturbed.
- **1994-06-06 — Digital Equipment Corp. v. Desktop Direct, Inc.; No. 93-405.** The interlocutory appeal remains dismissed. The district litigation continues in its existing posture. Final-judgment review and any independently available §1292(b) route remain subject to their own requirements; the Court neither issues a certificate nor grants appellate permission. No settlement performance, breach damages, sanction, rescission merits ruling, trademark judgment or additional Supreme Court stay is entered.
- **1994-06-06 — Farmer v. Brennan; No. 92-7247.** The case returns for reconsideration of the Rule 56(f) request and the merits judgment. Discovery may be allowed or denied on a proper basis; the merits must then be evaluated without an express-warning prerequisite. Defendants may assert individualized knowledge, causation, reasonable response and immunity defenses. Current prospective danger and the scope of any lawful protection remain for the lower court. No automatic trial, damages award or placement injunction is ordered.
- **1994-06-06 — Key Tronic Corp. v. United States; No. 93-376.** The case returns to the lower courts to implement the differentiated cost disposition. Litigation and the claimed negotiation expenses remain excluded; qualifying identification costs require task-specific proof and appropriate allocation. No exact recoverable fee amount is fixed. The conceded $4.2 million contribution bar, separately settled response costs and unappealed interest award remain undisturbed.
- **1994-06-13 — City of Ladue v. Gilleo, No. 92-1856; October Term 1993.** The judgment and existing protection against enforcement of the replacement prohibition stand. The City remains free to adopt otherwise constitutional size, number, placement, illumination and safety controls. The Court neither revives the former variance procedure nor decides the proposed six-square-foot substitute that was not argued to the district court. There is no new damages award, exemption-deletion order or fee disposition.
- **1994-06-13 — Department of Taxation and Finance of New York v. Milhelm Attea & Bros., Inc., No. 93-377; 512 U.S. 61.** The New York Court of Appeals is reversed on both grounds supporting its facial judgment, and the case is remanded for proceedings consistent with the limited rejection of facial preemption. The facial injunction cannot rest on those grounds. Concrete challenges to inadequate exempt supply, arbitrary approval or territory allocation, and excessive compliance burdens remain available on an actual record. No particular quota, tax collection from a tribe or treaty objection is adjudicated.
- **1994-06-13 — Howlett v. Birkdale Shipping Co., S.A., No. 93-670; 512 U.S. 92.** The Third Circuit's judgment is vacated and the case remanded for reconsideration of the turnover-warning claim on the full summary-judgment record under the stated duty. The lower courts must distinguish evidence of actual vessel knowledge from the condition facing a competent unloading stevedore and address the contrary visibility evidence. Renewed summary judgment remains possible; the Court orders neither trial, compensation, a negligence finding nor a finding of causation.
- **1994-06-13 — Ibanez v. Florida Department of Business and Professional Regulation, Board of Accountancy, No. 93-639; October Term 1993.** The case is remanded for proceedings consistent with reversal of the reprimand's CPA and CFP grounds. The Board's licensing jurisdiction remains conceded and intact. The Court prescribes no new disclaimer, finds no misconduct on the withdrawn unlicensed-firm count, revokes no credential and awards no damages. Future truthful or misleading representations and a different supported disclosure must be evaluated on their own records; no permanent immunity attaches to these initials.
- **1994-06-13 — Livadas v. Bradshaw, No. 92-1920; 512 U.S. 107.** The Ninth Circuit is reversed and the case remanded for proceedings consistent with the three holdings. The lower courts must determine appropriate source-authorized relief against the official-capacity policy, including the distinct monetary-relief and Eleventh Amendment constraints. No employer wage penalty, personal damages or automatic reinstatement of a money award is ordered; willfulness, backpay's effect and other underlying penalty defenses remain unresolved.
- **1994-06-13 — O’Melveny & Myers v. FDIC, No. 93-489; October Term 1993.** The case returns for consideration of California imputation, estoppel, professional duty and other preserved state-law questions. The unexplained original summary judgment is not automatically reinstated. No duty, breach, causation, loss amount, investor assignment, statute-of-frauds defense or adverse-interest exception is finally decided. The alleged client losses remain claims; an ADSB subsidiary's rescission payments to investors are not the reimbursement sought in this action. FIRREA's temporal reach remains open if a later necessary issue actually depends on it. No damages or refund is ordered.
- **1994-06-13 — Romano v. Oklahoma, No. 92-9093; 512 U.S. 1 (1994).** The case returns to the Oklahoma Court of Criminal Appeals with its judgment vacated only insofar as it sustains the Sarfaty death sentence. Further proceedings must respect the controlling responsibility holding and the distinction between prior conduct and another jury's death choice. The state court must address the remaining remedial issue under governing law; this Court has not resolved whether a new constitutional harmlessness determination may permit retention of the existing sentence or whether a new hearing must precede any continued pursuit of death. Neither proposed detailed instruction may be attributed to the Court. No guilt retrial, disposition of Thompson, particular replacement sentence, release or execution order is issued.
- **1994-06-13 — United States v. Carlton, No. 92-1941; 512 U.S. 26.** The Ninth Circuit is reversed and the case remanded for judgment consistent with applying the amended ownership rule. The estate receives no refund on the rejected due-process theory and no judicial compensation for its transaction loss. No new refund amount, penalty, valuation or allocation failure is found; the abandoned theories remain outside review.

## 6. Blockers and Revalidation Needs


The three carried chunk-1 blockers are resolved: Day and Sassower by express permanent scope closure without fee adjudication, and Cavanaugh by its November 30 merits Record. The scoped effective-date dependency check found no completed later adjudication requiring change; the existing Act No. 184 source admission remains unchanged. Historical frozen carryover lists describe earlier work states and are not current open-matter authority. The May 23, May 31, June 6, and June 13 inventory groups are complete. No chunk-6 matter is stopped. McDermott entered Boca Grande; Landgraf entered Rivers; Staples entered Posters in their express sequences. The completed Custis and other material earlier law entered Nichols. Preserve all other same-day baseline limits. The next Run must independently validate the next chunk's approval, sources, and dependencies.

## 7. Source and Research Cutoff

Each Record gives its own event-date cutoff and actual source limits. The current projection includes changes effective through June 13, 1994. The June 6 cases shared their start-of-day baseline, including with Nichols; the completed June 6 law entered June 13 only prospectively. The June 13 cases shared their own start-of-day baseline. Source retrieval and file preparation occurred in the September 27, 2026 operator session; those retrieval dates are not in-world law. See the [Run validation](../freeze/OT_1993CHUNK6_VALIDATION.md) for the checks actually completed and the Git checks deferred. The chunk-1 render now includes Cavanaugh and removes the three resolved Workflow Blockers; every other existing public entry is unchanged. The [resolution validation](../freeze/OT_1993CHUNK1_RESOLUTION_VALIDATION.md) records this bounded update and its checks.
